Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… what really made me come in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I work at a jewelry bench, so I’m used to needing to be careful and stay with tiny details, you know? But lately I’ll be in the middle of a work order and I’ll jump from answering a customer email, to noticing a saw mark on a piece, to some little noise outside the shop, and then suddenly I’m sitting there realizing I don’t even know where I left off.

It’s not just annoying—it’s starting to mess with my work. I’ll lose the thread of the whole job, like what the customer wanted or what step I was on, and then I have to backtrack and check everything again. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because my attention feels like it’s all over the place.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely restless. Like at the bench, I can usually sit there for hours doing tiny work, but lately I just… can’t stay put in the task. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.

And it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.

Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: What really brought me in is that my mind won’t stay on one thing anymore—at the bench I jump from a customer email to a saw mark to a noise outside and then I realize I’ve lost the whole work order.\n- key: S024\n  name: Depressed_Mood\n  description: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.\n  manifestation: These past couple weeks, I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the shine has gone out of everything, and I’ve cried in the truck before opening the shop.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: Even when I get in bed at a normal hour now, I toss around, wake up before dawn thinking about the missed delivery, and don’t feel rested when I drag myself up.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: My arms feel like lead at the bench, and polishing a setting or carrying a tray of stones wipes me out in a way a full night off doesn’t fix.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying every client promise I messed up and telling myself I’m a fraud who embarrassed my family over one award.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I’ll stare at a repair ticket or a tray of sapphires and can’t hold the measurements in my head long enough to decide what to do next.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I keep having thoughts that my family and my clients would be better off if I wasn’t around, and even though I don’t have a plan, it scares me how often it comes up.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: After the show I felt lit up and larger than life one minute, then snapped at anyone who asked me to stick to normal shop hours or slow down.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I couldn’t sit still at the bench; I’d bounce from sketching a new watch case to emailing suppliers to rearranging display trays long after closing.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: Winning that ribbon made me feel like I’d finally proved I was on another level, like I could take every custom order and turn each one into a showpiece no one else around here could touch.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: For a while I was sleeping maybe two or three hours, then getting up clear-headed and excited to start cutting stones or posting new designs before sunrise.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started lining up new commissions, messaging old customers, building a website plan, and sketching a whole Appalachian collection all at once, like I had to get it all moving right then.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I missed a major custom delivery and my shop records got so disorganized that I had to stop taking new work for now. My family has also been checking on me more because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The wired, out-of-control stretch started right after the jewelry-show award and went on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what really made me come in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I work at a jewelry bench, so I’m used to needing to be careful and stay with tiny details, you know? But lately I’ll be in the middle of a work order and I’ll jump from answering a customer email, to noticing a saw mark on a piece, to some little noise outside the shop, and then suddenly I’m sitting there realizing I don’t even know where I left off.\n\nIt’s not just annoying—it’s starting to mess with my work. I’ll lose the thread of the whole job, like what the customer wanted or what step I was on, and then I have to backtrack and check everything again. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because my attention feels like it’s all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself acting impulsively or feeling unusually restless or hyperactive along with your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely restless. Like at the bench, I can usually sit there for hours doing tiny work, but lately I just… can’t stay put in the task. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.\n\nAnd it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless in situations outside of work, like at home, while driving, or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be right in the middle of a customer’s job, something that should be step-by-step, and then I’m pulled into an email, then I spot some little saw mark or imperfection, then a noise outside catches me, and suddenly I’m sitting there like, wait, what was I doing?\n\nIt’s like I lose the whole thread of the work order. I have to go back and re-check what the customer asked for, what I already did, what step comes next… and that’s not normal for me. I’m used to tiny details being kind of my whole thing, so it’s been really unsettling. Outside of work I guess it’s harder for me to measure, but that same scattered feeling is what really made me come in.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or feeling restless when you were a child, like in school or during activities at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I’m… not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back on that because I figured you might ask something like that, but nothing really stands out clearly from when I was a kid.

I mean, school wasn’t my favorite thing, and I wasn’t some perfect student who loved sitting still all day, but I don’t remember it being like this—like where my attention just breaks apart and I can’t keep the thread of what I’m doing. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.

It’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.

My family has also been checking on me more because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The wired, out-of-control stretch started right after the jewelry-show award and went on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself acting impulsively or feeling unusually restless or hyperactive along with your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely restless. Like at the bench, I can usually sit there for hours doing tiny work, but lately I just… can’t stay put in the task. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.\n\nAnd it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless in situations outside of work, like at home, while driving, or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be right in the middle of a customer’s job, something that should be step-by-step, and then I’m pulled into an email, then I spot some little saw mark or imperfection, then a noise outside catches me, and suddenly I’m sitting there like, wait, what was I doing?\n\nIt’s like I lose the whole thread of the work order. I have to go back and re-check what the customer asked for, what I already did, what step comes next… and that’s not normal for me. I’m used to tiny details being kind of my whole thing, so it’s been really unsettling. Outside of work I guess it’s harder for me to measure, but that same scattered feeling is what really made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or feeling restless when you were a child, like in school or during activities at home?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure, honestly. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.

At the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.

That’s what brought me in, really. It’s not just normal boredom or zoning out during TV or chores or something. It’s that I’m doing detailed work where I’m supposed to be locked in, and instead I’m jumping from one thing to another and having to re-check the order because I can’t trust that I remember the sequence. It’s really frustrating.

I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nPatient: At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.\n\nThat’s what brought me in, really. It’s not just normal boredom or zoning out during TV or chores or something. It’s that I’m doing detailed work where I’m supposed to be locked in, and instead I’m jumping from one thing to another and having to re-check the order because I can’t trust that I remember the sequence. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m trying to think of a home example, and honestly… nothing at home jumps out as strongly as work does. I know you’re asking about chores or conversations, and I probably do get a little scattered there too, but it’s harder for me to point to one clean example and say, “Yes, that was just as bad.”

What really made me realize something was off was at the bench. I’ll be working on a customer’s piece, and my head just won’t stay with the job. I’m supposed to be following the order step by step, but then I check an email, then I notice some tiny saw mark or little imperfection, then a sound outside the shop pulls me away, and by the time I look back down I’m like… wait, where was I? What part of this did I already do? What did they ask for again?

That’s the part that scares me a little, because my work depends on keeping that thread. At home, if I lose track of something, it’s annoying, but at work it’s right in my face because I have to go back and reread the order and sort of rebuild the whole sequence in my head. So I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it just as clearly at home, but the work thing is very real and it’s not how my mind usually works.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. I’ve been trying not to make that sound bigger than it is, because I don’t want to be dramatic, but it’s been there. Especially these past couple weeks.

It’s not only work frustration. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in me. Like everything is technically the same—the house, the shop, the day ahead—but the shine has gone out of it. That’s the best way I can describe it, which sounds kind of fitting for what I do, I guess, but it really does feel like that. Things that usually have some color to them just feel flat.

And I’ve had mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before opening the shop, and I just… cry. Not because of one specific thing every time, not like I got a bad phone call or something happened right then. It’s more like I’m sitting there with my hand on the keys, knowing I need to go in and start the day, and I feel this wave of sadness or emptiness, and I don’t even know what to do with it. I’ll try to pull myself together because I can’t exactly walk in looking like that, but yeah, it’s been happening.

Like at the bench, I can usually sit there for hours doing tiny work, but lately I just… can’t stay put in the task. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.\n\nAnd it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless in situations outside of work, like at home, while driving, or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be right in the middle of a customer’s job, something that should be step-by-step, and then I’m pulled into an email, then I spot some little saw mark or imperfection, then a noise outside catches me, and suddenly I’m sitting there like, wait, what was I doing?\n\nIt’s like I lose the whole thread of the work order. I have to go back and re-check what the customer asked for, what I already did, what step comes next… and that’s not normal for me. I’m used to tiny details being kind of my whole thing, so it’s been really unsettling. Outside of work I guess it’s harder for me to measure, but that same scattered feeling is what really made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or feeling restless when you were a child, like in school or during activities at home?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back on that because I figured you might ask something like that, but nothing really stands out clearly from when I was a kid.\n\nI mean, school wasn’t my favorite thing, and I wasn’t some perfect student who loved sitting still all day, but I don’t remember it being like this—like where my attention just breaks apart and I can’t keep the thread of what I’m doing. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nPatient: At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.\n\nThat’s what brought me in, really. It’s not just normal boredom or zoning out during TV or chores or something. It’s that I’m doing detailed work where I’m supposed to be locked in, and instead I’m jumping from one thing to another and having to re-check the order because I can’t trust that I remember the sequence. It’s really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home, not at work, where you were doing something that needed focus—like a chore, a project, or even a conversation—and you found yourself getting just as distracted or restless as you’ve described at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a home example, and honestly… nothing at home jumps out as strongly as work does. I’ll be working on a customer’s piece, and my head just won’t stay with the job. I’m supposed to be following the order step by step, but then I check an email, then I notice some tiny saw mark or little imperfection, then a sound outside the shop pulls me away, and by the time I look back down I’m like… wait, where was I? What part of this did I already do? What did they ask for again?\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a little, because my work depends on keeping that thread. At home, if I lose track of something, it’s annoying, but at work it’s right in my face because I have to go back and reread the order and sort of rebuild the whole sequence in my head. So I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it just as clearly at home, but the work thing is very real and it’s not how my mind usually works.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it's not related to work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it like every single minute of every day, but these past couple weeks it’s been there most days.

I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nPatient: At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.\n\nThat’s what brought me in, really. It’s not just normal boredom or zoning out during TV or chores or something. It’s that I’m doing detailed work where I’m supposed to be locked in, and instead I’m jumping from one thing to another and having to re-check the order because I can’t trust that I remember the sequence. It’s really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home, not at work, where you were doing something that needed focus—like a chore, a project, or even a conversation—and you found yourself getting just as distracted or restless as you’ve described at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a home example, and honestly… nothing at home jumps out as strongly as work does. I know you’re asking about chores or conversations, and I probably do get a little scattered there too, but it’s harder for me to point to one clean example and say, “Yes, that was just as bad.”\n\nWhat really made me realize something was off was at the bench. I’ll be working on a customer’s piece, and my head just won’t stay with the job. I’m supposed to be following the order step by step, but then I check an email, then I notice some tiny saw mark or little imperfection, then a sound outside the shop pulls me away, and by the time I look back down I’m like… wait, where was I? What part of this did I already do? What did they ask for again?\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a little, because my work depends on keeping that thread. At home, if I lose track of something, it’s annoying, but at work it’s right in my face because I have to go back and reread the order and sort of rebuild the whole sequence in my head. So I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it just as clearly at home, but the work thing is very real and it’s not how my mind usually works.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it's not related to work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I’ve been trying not to make that sound bigger than it is, because I don’t want to be dramatic, but it’s been there. Especially these past couple weeks.\n\nIt’s not only work frustration. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in me. Like everything is technically the same—the house, the shop, the day ahead—but the shine has gone out of it. That’s the best way I can describe it, which sounds kind of fitting for what I do, I guess, but it really does feel like that. Things that usually have some color to them just feel flat.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before opening the shop, and I just… cry. Not because of one specific thing every time, not like I got a bad phone call or something happened right then. It’s more like I’m sitting there with my hand on the keys, knowing I need to go in and start the day, and I feel this wave of sadness or emptiness, and I don’t even know what to do with it. I’ll try to pull myself together because I can’t exactly walk in looking like that, but yeah, it’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Has this sadness or loss of interest been happening most days, and lasting throughout much of the day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it like every single minute of every day, but these past couple weeks it’s been there most days.\n\nIt’s especially right when I wake up. Before anything has even gone wrong, I’ll just have this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything has lost its shine. And then it kind of follows me into the day. Sometimes I can push through it because I have to open the shop and do what needs doing, but it doesn’t really feel like it lifts much.\n\nAnd like I said, there have been mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before going in, and I just start crying. Not because of one clear thing, just this wave of sadness or emptiness. So… yes, for at least the past couple weeks, most days, and a good chunk of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So… yes, for at least the past couple weeks, most days, and a good chunk of the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even activities that normally make you feel good?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressed_Mood\nmanifestation: \"These past couple weeks, I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like the shine has gone out of everything, and I’ve cried in the truck before opening the shop.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. It’s hard to explain because it’s not like I’ve sat down and made a list of, you know, “things I used to enjoy” and checked them off, but it feels like the feeling has drained out of a lot of things.

These past couple weeks especially, I wake up and there’s just this heavy, hollow feeling already there, before I’ve even done anything. Like the day hasn’t had a chance to go wrong yet, but it already feels dull. I keep thinking of it like the shine has gone out of everything, which I know sounds almost too on-the-nose because of my work, but that’s really what it feels like. Things that normally would give me some little spark or satisfaction just feel flat.

And it carries into the shop too. Usually there’s at least some pleasure in opening up, seeing the pieces, getting into the rhythm of the work. Even if it’s a hard day, there’s normally something grounding about it. Lately I’ll be sitting in my truck before going in, hand on the keys, and I just start crying. Not because I’m thinking, “Oh, I hate this specific thing,” but because everything feels empty and heavy, and I don’t feel the usual pull toward any of it. So… yes, I’d say I’m not getting the same interest or good feeling from things lately.

But lately I’ll be in the middle of a work order and I’ll jump from answering a customer email, to noticing a saw mark on a piece, to some little noise outside the shop, and then suddenly I’m sitting there realizing I don’t even know where I left off.\n\nIt’s not just annoying—it’s starting to mess with my work. I’ll lose the thread of the whole job, like what the customer wanted or what step I was on, and then I have to backtrack and check everything again. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.\n\nAnd it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless in situations outside of work, like at home, while driving, or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be right in the middle of a customer’s job, something that should be step-by-step, and then I’m pulled into an email, then I spot some little saw mark or imperfection, then a noise outside catches me, and suddenly I’m sitting there like, wait, what was I doing?\n\nIt’s like I lose the whole thread of the work order. I have to go back and re-check what the customer asked for, what I already did, what step comes next… and that’s not normal for me. I’m used to tiny details being kind of my whole thing, so it’s been really unsettling. Outside of work I guess it’s harder for me to measure, but that same scattered feeling is what really made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or feeling restless when you were a child, like in school or during activities at home?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back on that because I figured you might ask something like that, but nothing really stands out clearly from when I was a kid.\n\nI mean, school wasn’t my favorite thing, and I wasn’t some perfect student who loved sitting still all day, but I don’t remember it being like this—like where my attention just breaks apart and I can’t keep the thread of what I’m doing. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nPatient: At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.\n\nThat’s what brought me in, really. I’ll be working on a customer’s piece, and my head just won’t stay with the job. I’m supposed to be following the order step by step, but then I check an email, then I notice some tiny saw mark or little imperfection, then a sound outside the shop pulls me away, and by the time I look back down I’m like… wait, where was I? What part of this did I already do? What did they ask for again?\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a little, because my work depends on keeping that thread. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Yeah, that’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it’s not just being tired like, “Oh, I stayed up too late,” or “I had a long shift.” It feels heavier than that.

At the bench, my arms honestly feel like lead sometimes. Like I’ll be sitting there trying to do something that normally is just part of the rhythm—polishing a setting, holding a piece steady, even just moving through the steps—and it feels like I have to talk myself into each little motion. And I know that sounds dramatic because it’s not like I’m lifting furniture, but it feels like my body is moving through mud.

Even carrying a tray of stones, something I do all the time, can wipe me out in this weird way. I’ll set it down and feel like I need to sit there for a second, like I’ve spent way more energy than the task should’ve taken. And then I’ll think, “Okay, I just need a night off, I just need to rest,” but even when I do get that, it doesn’t really reset me. A full night off doesn’t fix it. I come back and still feel drained.

So… yeah, nearly every day lately, even small stuff feels like it takes more out of me than it should.

Yeah… it’s not really just little bursts. I mean, it may come in waves where I notice it harder, like first thing in the morning or right before I have to walk into the shop, but the feeling is sort of… there underneath everything for most of the day.

These past couple weeks especially, I wake up already feeling heavy and hollow, like before I’ve even had a chance to think about work or money or anything specific. It’s just there. And then I’ll go through the motions — get dressed, drive to the shop, sit there in the truck — and sometimes I’ve just started crying before opening up. Not because something happened right then, but because everything feels like it’s lost its shine.

So, yeah, I’d say it’s been nearly every day, and most of the day. It’s not only in one situation. The shop is where I notice it a lot because that’s where I spend so much time, but it starts before that and kind of follows me around.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.0, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what really made me come in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I work at a jewelry bench, so I’m used to needing to be careful and stay with tiny details, you know? But lately I’ll be in the middle of a work order and I’ll jump from answering a customer email, to noticing a saw mark on a piece, to some little noise outside the shop, and then suddenly I’m sitting there realizing I don’t even know where I left off.\n\nIt’s not just annoying—it’s starting to mess with my work. I’ll lose the thread of the whole job, like what the customer wanted or what step I was on, and then I have to backtrack and check everything again. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because my attention feels like it’s all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself acting impulsively or feeling unusually restless or hyperactive along with your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely restless. Like at the bench, I can usually sit there for hours doing tiny work, but lately I just… can’t stay put in the task. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.\n\nAnd it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless in situations outside of work, like at home, while driving, or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be right in the middle of a customer’s job, something that should be step-by-step, and then I’m pulled into an email, then I spot some little saw mark or imperfection, then a noise outside catches me, and suddenly I’m sitting there like, wait, what was I doing?\n\nIt’s like I lose the whole thread of the work order. I have to go back and re-check what the customer asked for, what I already did, what step comes next… and that’s not normal for me. I’m used to tiny details being kind of my whole thing, so it’s been really unsettling. Outside of work I guess it’s harder for me to measure, but that same scattered feeling is what really made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or feeling restless when you were a child, like in school or during activities at home?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back on that because I figured you might ask something like that, but nothing really stands out clearly from when I was a kid.\n\nI mean, school wasn’t my favorite thing, and I wasn’t some perfect student who loved sitting still all day, but I don’t remember it being like this—like where my attention just breaks apart and I can’t keep the thread of what I’m doing. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nPatient: At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.\n\nThat’s what brought me in, really. It’s not just normal boredom or zoning out during TV or chores or something. It’s that I’m doing detailed work where I’m supposed to be locked in, and instead I’m jumping from one thing to another and having to re-check the order because I can’t trust that I remember the sequence. It’s really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home, not at work, where you were doing something that needed focus—like a chore, a project, or even a conversation—and you found yourself getting just as distracted or restless as you’ve described at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a home example, and honestly… nothing at home jumps out as strongly as work does. I know you’re asking about chores or conversations, and I probably do get a little scattered there too, but it’s harder for me to point to one clean example and say, “Yes, that was just as bad.”\n\nWhat really made me realize something was off was at the bench. I’ll be working on a customer’s piece, and my head just won’t stay with the job. I’m supposed to be following the order step by step, but then I check an email, then I notice some tiny saw mark or little imperfection, then a sound outside the shop pulls me away, and by the time I look back down I’m like… wait, where was I? What part of this did I already do? What did they ask for again?\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a little, because my work depends on keeping that thread. At home, if I lose track of something, it’s annoying, but at work it’s right in my face because I have to go back and reread the order and sort of rebuild the whole sequence in my head. So I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it just as clearly at home, but the work thing is very real and it’s not how my mind usually works.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it's not related to work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I’ve been trying not to make that sound bigger than it is, because I don’t want to be dramatic, but it’s been there. Especially these past couple weeks.\n\nIt’s not only work frustration. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in me. Like everything is technically the same—the house, the shop, the day ahead—but the shine has gone out of it. That’s the best way I can describe it, which sounds kind of fitting for what I do, I guess, but it really does feel like that. Things that usually have some color to them just feel flat.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before opening the shop, and I just… cry. Not because of one specific thing every time, not like I got a bad phone call or something happened right then. It’s more like I’m sitting there with my hand on the keys, knowing I need to go in and start the day, and I feel this wave of sadness or emptiness, and I don’t even know what to do with it. I’ll try to pull myself together because I can’t exactly walk in looking like that, but yeah, it’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Has this sadness or loss of interest been happening most days, and lasting throughout much of the day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it like every single minute of every day, but these past couple weeks it’s been there most days.\n\nIt’s especially right when I wake up. Before anything has even gone wrong, I’ll just have this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything has lost its shine. And then it kind of follows me into the day. Sometimes I can push through it because I have to open the shop and do what needs doing, but it doesn’t really feel like it lifts much.\n\nAnd like I said, there have been mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before going in, and I just start crying. Not because of one clear thing, just this wave of sadness or emptiness. So… yes, for at least the past couple weeks, most days, and a good chunk of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even activities that normally make you feel good?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s hard to explain because it’s not like I’ve sat down and made a list of, you know, “things I used to enjoy” and checked them off, but it feels like the feeling has drained out of a lot of things.\n\nThese past couple weeks especially, I wake up and there’s just this heavy, hollow feeling already there, before I’ve even done anything. Like the day hasn’t had a chance to go wrong yet, but it already feels dull. I keep thinking of it like the shine has gone out of everything, which I know sounds almost too on-the-nose because of my work, but that’s really what it feels like. Things that normally would give me some little spark or satisfaction just feel flat.\n\nAnd it carries into the shop too. Usually there’s at least some pleasure in opening up, seeing the pieces, getting into the rhythm of the work. Even if it’s a hard day, there’s normally something grounding about it. Lately I’ll be sitting in my truck before going in, hand on the keys, and I just start crying. Not because I’m thinking, “Oh, I hate this specific thing,” but because everything feels empty and heavy, and I don’t feel the usual pull toward any of it. So… yes, I’d say I’m not getting the same interest or good feeling from things lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t have much energy or that even small tasks take a lot out of you, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, that’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it’s not just being tired like, “Oh, I stayed up too late,” or “I had a long shift.” It feels heavier than that.\n\nAt the bench, my arms honestly feel like lead sometimes. Like I’ll be sitting there trying to do something that normally is just part of the rhythm—polishing a setting, holding a piece steady, even just moving through the steps—and it feels like I have to talk myself into each little motion. And I know that sounds dramatic because it’s not like I’m lifting furniture, but it feels like my body is moving through mud.\n\nEven carrying a tray of stones, something I do all the time, can wipe me out in this weird way. I’ll set it down and feel like I need to sit there for a second, like I’ve spent way more energy than the task should’ve taken. And then I’ll think, “Okay, I just need a night off, I just need to rest,” but even when I do get that, it doesn’t really reset me. A full night off doesn’t fix it. I come back and still feel drained.\n\nSo… yeah, nearly every day lately, even small stuff feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way — like things have lost their shine or you’re just going through the motions — for most of the day, nearly every day, and not just in short bursts or certain situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really just little bursts. I mean, it may come in waves where I notice it harder, like first thing in the morning or right before I have to walk into the shop, but the feeling is sort of… there underneath everything for most of the day.\n\nThese past couple weeks especially, I wake up already feeling heavy and hollow, like before I’ve even had a chance to think about work or money or anything specific. It’s just there. And then I’ll go through the motions — get dressed, drive to the shop, sit there in the truck — and sometimes I’ve just started crying before opening up. Not because something happened right then, but because everything feels like it’s lost its shine.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s been nearly every day, and most of the day. It’s not only in one situation. The shop is where I notice it a lot because that’s where I spend so much time, but it starts before that and kind of follows me around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The inattentive and restless symptoms remain consistent with F90.0 (ADHD - Predominantly Inattentive Type). There is no strong evidence for hyperactivity or impulsivity onset in childhood, which limits the likelihood of F90.2. I mean… I’m not totally sure how to answer that differently than before. It’s just a lot less than normal.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less or much more than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight or anything, and I don’t think I’ve noticed some big obvious change where my clothes fit totally different or people are commenting on it.

Appetite-wise… it’s kind of hard for me to tell because my eating is already not super structured. Like with work, I might get busy and forget lunch, or I’ll just grab something quick, so I don’t always have a clean “normal” to compare it to. I don’t think I’ve suddenly been eating way more or way less in a way that stands out to me, though.

Some days food doesn’t sound that appealing, but I still eat something eventually. Other days I eat more normally. So… yeah, I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I’ve noticed with appetite or weight.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what really made me come in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I work at a jewelry bench, so I’m used to needing to be careful and stay with tiny details, you know? But lately I’ll be in the middle of a work order and I’ll jump from answering a customer email, to noticing a saw mark on a piece, to some little noise outside the shop, and then suddenly I’m sitting there realizing I don’t even know where I left off.\n\nIt’s not just annoying—it’s starting to mess with my work. I’ll lose the thread of the whole job, like what the customer wanted or what step I was on, and then I have to backtrack and check everything again. That’s really what scared me enough to come in, because my attention feels like it’s all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself acting impulsively or feeling unusually restless or hyperactive along with your difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely restless. Like at the bench, I can usually sit there for hours doing tiny work, but lately I just… can’t stay put in the task. I’ll start sketching out an idea for a new watch case, and before that’s even really formed I’m suddenly emailing suppliers about materials, and then I’ll notice the display trays look wrong or messy and I’ll start rearranging them.\n\nAnd it’s not even during normal hours sometimes. I’ll be there after closing, when I should be wrapping up, and I’m still moving around doing all these little things like they’re urgent. It feels like my body and my brain are both pushing me from one thing to the next, and I don’t really settle back down into the actual work I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless in situations outside of work, like at home, while driving, or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the biggest, clearest place I notice it is at work, because it’s so obvious there. Like I said, at the bench my mind just won’t stay on one thing. I’ll be right in the middle of a customer’s job, something that should be step-by-step, and then I’m pulled into an email, then I spot some little saw mark or imperfection, then a noise outside catches me, and suddenly I’m sitting there like, wait, what was I doing?\n\nIt’s like I lose the whole thread of the work order. I have to go back and re-check what the customer asked for, what I already did, what step comes next… and that’s not normal for me. I’m used to tiny details being kind of my whole thing, so it’s been really unsettling. Outside of work I guess it’s harder for me to measure, but that same scattered feeling is what really made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or feeling restless when you were a child, like in school or during activities at home?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to think back on that because I figured you might ask something like that, but nothing really stands out clearly from when I was a kid.\n\nI mean, school wasn’t my favorite thing, and I wasn’t some perfect student who loved sitting still all day, but I don’t remember it being like this—like where my attention just breaks apart and I can’t keep the thread of what I’m doing. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers saying I was constantly restless or couldn’t focus, at least not in a way that stuck with me.\n\nIt’s possible there were little things, you know, like any kid getting bored or distracted, but I can’t point to something and say, “Yeah, that was the same problem back then.” This feels much more noticeable to me now because it’s getting in the way of work in a way I can actually see.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting distracted or feeling restless at home, while doing chores, watching TV, or talking with family or friends?\n\nPatient: At home it’s… I guess I notice it some, but the thing that really makes it obvious is still work. Like, the best way I can describe it is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nAt the bench, I’ll be trying to follow a work order, and then I’m suddenly looking at a customer email, then I notice a tiny saw mark or some imperfection and get pulled into that, then there’s a noise outside or in the shop and I’m distracted by that. And then I kind of come back to myself and realize I don’t even know exactly where I left off. Like I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was doing.\n\nThat’s what brought me in, really. It’s not just normal boredom or zoning out during TV or chores or something. It’s that I’m doing detailed work where I’m supposed to be locked in, and instead I’m jumping from one thing to another and having to re-check the order because I can’t trust that I remember the sequence. It’s really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were at home, not at work, where you were doing something that needed focus—like a chore, a project, or even a conversation—and you found yourself getting just as distracted or restless as you’ve described at work?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a home example, and honestly… nothing at home jumps out as strongly as work does. I know you’re asking about chores or conversations, and I probably do get a little scattered there too, but it’s harder for me to point to one clean example and say, “Yes, that was just as bad.”\n\nWhat really made me realize something was off was at the bench. I’ll be working on a customer’s piece, and my head just won’t stay with the job. I’m supposed to be following the order step by step, but then I check an email, then I notice some tiny saw mark or little imperfection, then a sound outside the shop pulls me away, and by the time I look back down I’m like… wait, where was I? What part of this did I already do? What did they ask for again?\n\nThat’s the part that scares me a little, because my work depends on keeping that thread. At home, if I lose track of something, it’s annoying, but at work it’s right in my face because I have to go back and reread the order and sort of rebuild the whole sequence in my head. So I can’t honestly say I’ve noticed it just as clearly at home, but the work thing is very real and it’s not how my mind usually works.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, sad, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, even when it's not related to work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I’ve been trying not to make that sound bigger than it is, because I don’t want to be dramatic, but it’s been there. Especially these past couple weeks.\n\nIt’s not only work frustration. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, there’s this heavy, hollow feeling in me. Like everything is technically the same—the house, the shop, the day ahead—but the shine has gone out of it. That’s the best way I can describe it, which sounds kind of fitting for what I do, I guess, but it really does feel like that. Things that usually have some color to them just feel flat.\n\nAnd I’ve had mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before opening the shop, and I just… cry. Not because of one specific thing every time, not like I got a bad phone call or something happened right then. It’s more like I’m sitting there with my hand on the keys, knowing I need to go in and start the day, and I feel this wave of sadness or emptiness, and I don’t even know what to do with it. I’ll try to pull myself together because I can’t exactly walk in looking like that, but yeah, it’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Has this sadness or loss of interest been happening most days, and lasting throughout much of the day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it like every single minute of every day, but these past couple weeks it’s been there most days.\n\nIt’s especially right when I wake up. Before anything has even gone wrong, I’ll just have this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything has lost its shine. And then it kind of follows me into the day. Sometimes I can push through it because I have to open the shop and do what needs doing, but it doesn’t really feel like it lifts much.\n\nAnd like I said, there have been mornings where I’m sitting in my truck before going in, and I just start crying. Not because of one clear thing, just this wave of sadness or emptiness. So… yes, for at least the past couple weeks, most days, and a good chunk of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even activities that normally make you feel good?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. It’s hard to explain because it’s not like I’ve sat down and made a list of, you know, “things I used to enjoy” and checked them off, but it feels like the feeling has drained out of a lot of things.\n\nThese past couple weeks especially, I wake up and there’s just this heavy, hollow feeling already there, before I’ve even done anything. Like the day hasn’t had a chance to go wrong yet, but it already feels dull. I keep thinking of it like the shine has gone out of everything, which I know sounds almost too on-the-nose because of my work, but that’s really what it feels like. Things that normally would give me some little spark or satisfaction just feel flat.\n\nAnd it carries into the shop too. Usually there’s at least some pleasure in opening up, seeing the pieces, getting into the rhythm of the work. Even if it’s a hard day, there’s normally something grounding about it. Lately I’ll be sitting in my truck before going in, hand on the keys, and I just start crying. Not because I’m thinking, “Oh, I hate this specific thing,” but because everything feels empty and heavy, and I don’t feel the usual pull toward any of it. So… yes, I’d say I’m not getting the same interest or good feeling from things lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t have much energy or that even small tasks take a lot out of you, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Yeah, that’s actually been one of the more frustrating parts, because it’s not just being tired like, “Oh, I stayed up too late,” or “I had a long shift.” It feels heavier than that.\n\nAt the bench, my arms honestly feel like lead sometimes. Like I’ll be sitting there trying to do something that normally is just part of the rhythm—polishing a setting, holding a piece steady, even just moving through the steps—and it feels like I have to talk myself into each little motion. And I know that sounds dramatic because it’s not like I’m lifting furniture, but it feels like my body is moving through mud.\n\nEven carrying a tray of stones, something I do all the time, can wipe me out in this weird way. I’ll set it down and feel like I need to sit there for a second, like I’ve spent way more energy than the task should’ve taken. And then I’ll think, “Okay, I just need a night off, I just need to rest,” but even when I do get that, it doesn’t really reset me. A full night off doesn’t fix it. I come back and still feel drained.\n\nSo… yeah, nearly every day lately, even small stuff feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way — like things have lost their shine or you’re just going through the motions — for most of the day, nearly every day, and not just in short bursts or certain situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not really just little bursts. I mean, it may come in waves where I notice it harder, like first thing in the morning or right before I have to walk into the shop, but the feeling is sort of… there underneath everything for most of the day.\n\nThese past couple weeks especially, I wake up already feeling heavy and hollow, like before I’ve even had a chance to think about work or money or anything specific. It’s just there. And then I’ll go through the motions — get dressed, drive to the shop, sit there in the truck — and sometimes I’ve just started crying before opening up. Not because something happened right then, but because everything feels like it’s lost its shine.\n\nSo, yeah, I’d say it’s been nearly every day, and most of the day. It’s not only in one situation. The shop is where I notice it a lot because that’s where I spend so much time, but it starts before that and kind of follows me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take any pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even activities you normally looked forward to?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’m not totally sure how to answer that differently than before. I guess I have noticed some of that, yeah, but I don’t know if I’d say “don’t take any pleasure” like it’s completely zero all the time. It’s more like things feel muted, like I can remember that I’m supposed to enjoy them, or that I used to look forward to them, but in the moment it doesn’t really land the same way.\n\nLike, I’ll go toward something out of habit, because normally it would feel good or at least feel like me, and then I’m just sort of there doing it without much feeling behind it. It’s not always that I hate it or anything. It’s more disappointing than that, honestly, because I keep waiting for the familiar good part to kick in and it just… doesn’t really.\n\nSo, yeah, I’ve noticed a loss of interest or pleasure, but I don’t know if I’d say I have absolutely none. It’s just a lot less than normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less or much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know, honestly. I haven’t been tracking my weight or anything, and I don’t think I’ve noticed some big obvious change where my clothes fit totally different or people are commenting on it.\n\nAppetite-wise… it’s kind of hard for me to tell because my eating is already not super structured. Like with work, I might get busy and forget lunch, or I’ll just grab something quick, so I don’t always have a clean “normal” to compare it to. I don’t think I’ve suddenly been eating way more or way less in a way that stands out to me, though.\n\nSome days food doesn’t sound that appealing, but I still eat something eventually. Other days I eat more normally. So… yeah, I’m not sure. Nothing dramatic that I’ve noticed with appetite or weight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. That’s actually been pretty loud in my head.

I keep going back over every promise I made to clients that I didn’t follow through on the way I should have — like jobs I said would be ready, calls I should’ve returned sooner, little things that maybe someone else would say are normal mistakes, but I don’t hear them that way. I hear it like proof that I’m not who people thought I was.

And I keep telling myself I’m a fraud. Like I got this award and people made a big deal out of it, and my family was proud, and now it feels like I embarrassed them for even letting them believe that meant something. It’s like I keep thinking, “You had one good moment and then everyone saw you weren’t actually that good.” I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels completely convincing.

It’s not even one mistake. It’s like my brain lines them all up — every client I disappointed, every promise I made too confidently — and then it turns into this whole story that I don’t deserve the respect people gave me. So yeah, I’ve been blaming myself a lot more than usual. Even for things that maybe weren’t fully my fault, I still end up feeling like they were.
